# Escapes — defects that got past a green gate

[Documentation](../INDEX.md) / [Operations](README.md)

Same rule as any repo running this process: a defect found *after* its ticket passed means **a gate
said yes when it should have said no**. The bug is the small half; the check that let it through is
the real defect. Fixing it is not the deliverable — changing the check so the class cannot pass again
is.

Each escape files a `FIX` with `escaped_from:`, answers §3's four questions, lands the structural
change in the SAME commit, and adds a row here.

## Ledger

| 2026-10-20 | **A passed ticket whose work has landed keeps both of its lanes for ever, because their sessions finished their turns and never exited.** `TL-FEAT-041` on the instance pointed at `navo-platform`: `status: pass`, squashed onto `main` as `650bfebb`, and twenty-plus minutes later `lane/20 run idle … ended` and `lane/22 qa idle … ended` still held their seats, their bwrap'd sessions up for 31 and 23 minutes. `GET /api/tickets/TL-FEAT-041` answered `delivery: running — "lane lane/20 holds it and its agent is alive"`, `claimed: pass`, `disagrees: true`. `api::liveLanes` counts `idle` as alive so delivery never reaches `done`, and `releaseLandedLanes` fires only on `done` and skips any live pid on top. | `SRV-FEAT-415` | the operator, 2026-10-20 — *"a ticket finished and its lanes were never released"* | **The fixture built the one state the product never ends a round in.** `test_a_lane_whose_work_has_landed_is_released` claims a lane and *"no run ever started in it"*; every real round ends with a role session that wrote its verdict, finished its turn and stayed up — `land.md`'s first paragraph says so. The criterion was phrased about what the fixture could build (*"no process alive in it"*) and the code then gated on that phrase twice. The structural change owed in the same commit, in `_TEMPLATE.md` §1: a criterion about what happens after a round ends is asserted on a lane whose session ran and finished its turn — a lane that never started a run may be one of the kinds, never the only one. |

| 2026-09-10 | **A lane of `factory-server` could run neither the gate `.factory/factory.json` declares for it nor the agent it declares, and `test_confinement_does_not_break_normal_work` — the case §1 says *"will actually decide the design"* — was green over it for six weeks.** The declared gate is `cd server && pytest tests/ -v` and the declared agent is `claude`; on an ordinary workstation both resolve only under the developer's home, which a lane deliberately does not get, and nothing put a declared toolchain on a lane's `PATH`. Measured from inside `lane/11`: `which claude npm pytest` → exit 1, and `cd server && python3 -m pytest tests/ -v` → `No module named pytest`. The product had been printing the diagnosis at every manifest load — `command_unrunnable`, *"Declare where \"pytest\" is in workspaces.factory-server.toolchain"* — and no gate read a warning. Underneath it, the same `p.writable` list held host paths and the two paths the confinement synthesises, so a factory pointed at itself from inside its own lane refused **every** lane with *"writable path /tmp/factory is above the repository root"*, and the lane's tmpfs home landed on top of the enclosing lane's home — turning `test_write_outside_lane_is_refused`, the assertion the whole ticket exists for, into a write that exits `0`. | SRV-FEAT-002 | SRV-FEAT-002 QA round 2, 2026-09-07, from inside a real factory lane | **The negative declared its own gate.** `sh ./gate.sh` is `mktemp`, `cc`, `git` and `python3` — every one of which arrives with `--ro-bind /usr /usr` — so the case never asked about a program living where real toolchains live, and it passed on every machine while the behaviour under it was false. That is this ledger's *"a test that injects convenient inputs instead of the module's real defaults"*, arriving in the ticket whose own §3 wrote the rule. Two further reasons it stayed hidden: every case ran on a developer workstation, where the confinement's synthesised paths collide with nothing, and the two argv guards that would catch a widening both call `requires_real_agent()`, so inside a lane they are RED and assert nothing. | SRV-FEAT-002 round 3: the `Policy` separates host binds from the paths it synthesises (`writableInside`), those paths are **invented** rather than named so they cannot land on a directory the world already owns, `PATH` is built by the confinement from the declaration (`/factory/bin/<agent>`, every declared toolchain directory holding an executable, then the inherited entries that exist inside the namespace), the confinement publishes `FACTORY_LANE_HOME` so the home guard cannot fail open where passwd knows nothing about lanes, and `.factory/factory.json` answers the warning the product was already printing. Seven new cases, five watched RED; the two mandatory negatives (`test_a_lane_sees_no_toolchain_it_did_not_declare`, `test_a_lane_cannot_reach_a_program_its_workspace_did_not_declare`) are what stop the cheap fix, and `test_making_a_declared_toolchain_reachable_adds_no_bind` asks the argv guards' question in a form a lane without credentials can run. |
| 2026-09-08 | **The squash author is decided by a tie, and the tie goes to whoever committed last — which is always the QA session.** `lander.cpp:347-349` seeds the winner with `order.front()` and replaces it only on a strict `>`; `order` is filled from `git log --no-merges --format='%an <%ae>' <base>..<tip>`, which prints newest first. Measured by hand against the real server on a fixture where the run lane made one commit and the QA lane one: `47a62e2  Reviewer Three <reviewer@example.invalid>  |  factory  |  DRV-1: A thing a QA lane passed` — `Member One` wrote the work, the reviewer wrote the verdict, and the member's work is now the reviewer's. The defect lives only where the counts are EQUAL and no fixture has ever had equal counts: the same shape as *a value aggregated from a collection is asserted on a collection that disagrees with itself*, one step along — a reduction whose interesting case is the tie, measured only where there is a winner. `SRV-FIX-475` added three worlds where the run and role branches disagree and none of them makes their commit COUNTS agree. | `SRV-FIX-477`, filed with this row, **not yet landed**: the tie goes to the branch's own work and the reviewer becomes a `Co-Authored-By:` trailer, with a case whose fixture asserts the two counts are equal before it asserts anything about the product, a majority case in both directions so the fix cannot be *always prefer the oldest*, and a negative that no landing is ever authored by the tool. Structural, owed in the same commit: `server/design/land.md` gains **every reduction over a branch's commits states what it does when the inputs TIE, and the case that evidences it is a tie**. | 2026-09-08 | **A QA lane's PASS never lands, because the lander reads the verdict and `status: pass` from the run branch and a QA lane cannot write there.** The first time `app/land` ran against real QA output — the operator's session, 2026-09-08 14:57–15:01 — three tickets sat at `PASS WITH MINORS` with `status: pass` committed and pushed, and it landed none of them: `land: SRV-FIX-443 — docs/tickets/SRV-FIX-443-…md on ticket/SRV-FIX-443 does not say status: pass`, `land: SRV-FIX-470 — no QA verdict on ticket/SRV-FIX-470`, `land: WEB-FEAT-366 — no QA verdict on ticket/WEB-FEAT-366`. Every one of the three verdicts was on the QA lane's own branch (`ticket/<id>-qa` at `7b1aad34`, `00ad0d36`, `f7cc6607`). The QA lane cannot move `ticket/<id>` — it is checked out in the run lane's worktree, `S4` confines the lane to its own worktree, and a push to `origin/ticket/<id>` moves nothing the lander resolves — so the only actors who can are the run lane's stopped session and a person. Every run the tool itself starts happens in a lane, where the second copy is not on the filesystem at all (`find / -name qa-ticket.md` returns only this checkout's), so the rule reported success from the one placement that cannot ask it, through two QA rounds, while `qa-ticket.md` differed the whole time. | `SRV-FIX-532` round 2 | the operator, 2026-09-12, running `pytest tests/ -m repository` from the developer's checkout against this branch — logs committed at `d7d08543`: `['qa-ticket.md']`, and criterion G's own case naming it as the one owned red of eleven | **The round that fixed *green only in lanes* was itself checked only in lanes** — this ledger's `SRV-FIX-525` row, one day later and one rule over. The tier table names the module a diff edited; nothing names the PLACEMENT a check needs in order to be asked, and a lane is the only placement `run-ticket` can produce. The cause had two sites one screen apart (`::test_a_ticket_of_the_undeclared_repo_is_not_reachable` is the second), which is this ledger's most repeated shape: a class fixed at the site the verdict named. | `SRV-FIX-532` round 3, landed with this row: both sites now `pytest.fail` with the reason, the way `test_lane_home.py::skipped` already does, so *not asked* is reported AND red; `test_catalog.py::test_a_rule_that_could_not_be_asked_never_reports_a_pass` sweeps this file's syntax for the class — a `test_*` that binds the helper's answer and ends its `is None` branch without an assert, raise or `pytest.fail` — and `::test_the_unasked_rule_sweep_reads_the_branch_and_not_the_wording` is the mandatory negative over three members: the shipped shape is named, both honest endings are not, and a file that never asks sweeps to nothing. The byte-equality itself stays owed by `SRV-FIX-527`, whose §1 already assigns it — one `cp` in the enclosing repository, by the person landing, never by a lane. |


## Patterns to watch for

- **A fix for a class of defect is not finished until the same shape has been looked for in the TEST
  FACTORY.** `SRV-FIX-038` closed "a ticket id matches any process on the machine" in `probe.cpp`, argued it
  well, and the reasoning never reached `conftest.py` — which did the same thing with a path and, unlike the
  product, was allowed to `kill`. Scaffolding is exempt from review by habit and is the only code in the
  repository that signals processes. When a class is closed, grep the suite for it too.
- **A defect that only appears under load is not thereby a flake; it is a defect with a concurrency term in
  it.** "The machine was busy" reads as an excuse rather than a hypothesis, and that reflex set aside
  `SRV-FIX-042` and `SRV-FIX-058` twice each and hid `SRV-FIX-062` through three destroyed agent runs. The
  correlation was in the timestamps from the first one onward.

- **A red capture is evidence only if every line in it is attributed.** A RED run is committed to
  prove a feature is missing, so its failures are *expected* — which is exactly the place an
  unrelated failure can sit unnoticed and be merged. `SRV-FIX-034` spent two days and eight tickets
  in `SRV-FIX-030`'s red log. Before committing a red capture, account for every FAILED line: the
  ones this ticket is about, and — separately named — the ones it is not.
- **"It passed" is not a measurement when the test is timing-dependent.** A test that is green
  nineteen times in twenty reads exactly like a test that is green, and the first real regression it
  catches gets re-run rather than read. Anything that waits, polls, forks or races is not proven by
  one green run: `tests/soak.sh` exists so the question costs one command, and the answer belongs in
  the report next to the result.
- **A variable that redirects an external program's configuration moves everything that program
  resolves from it, not the one thing you aimed it at.** `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` was set to fix
  credentials, the credentials were checked, and the trust store went with it in silence for a
  day. The check that would have caught it is not a better test of the thing you aimed at — it is
  an **enumeration of what moved**, written where the redirection happens and backed by one
  measurement per entry. And when the same redirection makes a second file with the same name as
  one the developer already has, assume the tool is reading the wrong one until a capture says
  otherwise: here the file the tool depended on had been created by the previous ticket's own
  measurement, and nobody had opened it.
- **A dropped permission is not an error, it is a question — and a question looks like the
  product working.** Every gate on the lane asked whether the agent answered, and an agent with
  no permissions answers perfectly. When a feature's failure mode is *more prompts* rather than
  *an error*, no assertion about liveness can see it; the assertion has to be about what the
  agent GOT — a capability only the dropped declaration grants, exercised, with the negative
  measured too. Asserting the absence of a warning string is the same trap one layer up: it
  passes the day the dependency rewords it.
- **A test that waits for one fact and asserts about another.** `WEB-FIX-031` waited for the element
  and asserted about its data; `SRV-FIX-034` waited for the parent's line and asserted about the
  child's existence. Both passed for as long as something slow happened to sit in the gap. The wait
  and the assertion have to be about the **same event** — and the strongest way to get there is a
  signal only the thing under test could have produced, so the ordering is causal rather than lucky.

- **A generated artefact checked only against its source.** `tokens_match_design`, the socket types, the HTTP types: each guarantees the client agrees with the thing it was generated from, and none of them can notice that the source is wrong. Agreement is not quality. Somewhere in each generated mechanism there has to be one assertion about the WORLD — a rendered colour pair, a live response, a real browser — and not about the agreement.
- **A check that skips what it cannot read.** An exemption list is at least visible; `if (x === null) continue` is an exemption nobody wrote down, and it excused four of seven state tones from the contrast gate for as long as the gate existed. Unparseable input is a failure, never a pass.
- **A boundary that was described but never asserted.** The lane model was written down, believed,
  and never tried. A rule the agent is *asked* to follow is not a boundary either — the agent that
  escaped was following a prompt telling it to stay in its lane. If the claim is "X cannot happen",
  the gate is an attempt to make X happen.
- **A refusal that succeeds somewhere harmless is not a refusal.** The first working sandbox in
  `SRV-FEAT-002` let `echo x > <main-checkout>/PWNED` return **0**: the write landed in an ephemeral
  directory the sandbox helper had created to hold a mount, and vanished with the process. Every
  assertion of the form *"the file did not appear"* and *"`git status` is unchanged"* passed
  perfectly. It is the worse outcome, not the safer one — the process believes it wrote the file and
  reports success to a human. When testing a boundary, assert that the operation **failed**, not only
  that the far side is unchanged; and check what your isolation mechanism silently provides to make
  itself work, because that is where the hole will be.
- **An instruction that contradicts a containment model.** The instruction wins, every time — it is
  specific, it is in the document the agent was told to follow, and the model is usually somewhere
  else. Instructions are part of the containment model and have to be read as carefully as the code.
- **A test that reaches the wrong server passes for the wrong reason.** The first red run of
  `SRV-FEAT-001` reported a route being refused by a server that did not exist yet — the deleted
  Python prototype was still listening on the port two hours later. A red run has to be read as
  carefully as a green one: check *why* it failed, not just that it did.
- **A prototype's assumptions surviving into the product.** The prototype was deleted rather than
  ported for exactly this reason.
- **A tool that reports nothing has not said the code is correct.** ThreadSanitizer on an asio
  thread pool under-reports: the scheduler's own mutex manufactures happens-before between handlers
  that merely ran at different moments, so unguarded state only produces a report when two handlers
  touch it at literally the same instant. `SRV-FIX-024`'s race needed a 400 ms window held open
  before it appeared at all — 0 reports in 25 runs without it, 4 in 4 with it. A green sanitizer run
  is evidence about the interleavings that happened, not the ones that are possible. When a criterion
  is "the tool reports nothing", ask first what that tool is structurally unable to see, and write
  the answer next to the result.
- **A gate that only looks from inside cannot see the shape of the door.** When a specification
  states a property about *exposure* — what is reachable, what is writable, who may call — the
  criterion has to be written from outside the process. From inside, every one of those properties
  looks satisfied: a `0.0.0.0` listener answers `127.0.0.1` perfectly.
- **A check that names the things it knows about.** Asserting ports 7777 and 7778 are loopback would
  have passed a server that opened a third. Ask the system what exists and judge all of it, so the
  check covers what the next ticket adds without anyone remembering to extend it.
- **A default inherited from a product with different needs.** `tcp::v4()` is correct in a server
  that serves a network and wrong in a tool whose security model is a sentence about loopback. Code
  copied from a healthy neighbour carries that neighbour's assumptions, and those are exactly the
  ones nobody re-reads.
- **A claim that a check generalises is itself a criterion, and it needs a test.** "This will catch the next one too" is the sentence that stops the next person looking, and it is almost never verified — the check gets written against the defect that just happened and described as though it were general. If a ticket says its assertion covers a class, open one other member of that class and watch the assertion catch it.
- **A convention copied from another codebase arrives without the facts that made it correct.** "Never a mutex in event-driven code" is right for a single-threaded reactor and this engine runs four threads; `tcp::v4()` is right for a server that serves a network and wrong for a loopback tool. When a rule is inherited, the thing to verify is not the rule — it is the assumption underneath it that nobody wrote down.
- **Vendored code arrives with its origin's test coverage, not yours.** `core/` was adopted whole
  because the product it came from runs fine — but "runs fine" is a statement about the paths that
  product exercises. Its blind spots come across with the code and are invisible, because nothing in
  the new repository ever looked there either. When code is adopted from elsewhere, the question is
  not "does it work" but **which of its paths has anyone ever asserted anything about** — and
  shutdown, teardown and restart are the usual answer to "none of them".
- **A path no test takes is a path with no behaviour.** Every test here ended its server with
  `proc.kill()`, so for a year the shutdown path had no gate of any kind and a one-in-three race
  lived in it. Convenience in a fixture teardown decides what the suite is *able* to notice. If the
  product has a lifecycle, the fixture has to use it — a teardown that skips the code under test is
  not a teardown, it is a hole with a green tick over it.
- **Fixing a rule where it was noticed, not where it lives.** The same "commit on `main`" sentence has now been found three times in three files. A rule lives in every file that quotes it; correcting one copy leaves the others to be obeyed. Grep the repo for the wording before calling it fixed.
- **A convenience default is an invitation, and the machine always accepts.** `localhost:7777` in a
  test, `?? '7777'` in a dev proxy, `|| 'http://127.0.0.1:7780'` in a gate — each one was written so
  the common case needs no typing, and each one silently means *whatever is on that port will do*.
  The cost only appears when a second thing exists: a second checkout, a second session, a leftover
  process. The fix is not a better default but no default: start what you talk to, or be told what
  to talk to, and fail loudly when neither happened.
- **A pattern in this file with no gate is a pattern that keeps happening.** "A test that reaches the wrong server passes for the wrong reason" was written down after the first incident and then cost three more sessions — everyone knew, everyone worked around it, and the next person paid again. Writing down how a gate lies does not stop it lying; only a check does. When a row goes in this ledger, name in the same commit the assertion that now makes it impossible — and if there isn't one, that is the ticket.
- **A sandbox that reports success for a write it silently redirected.** `SRV-FEAT-002`'s first mount namespace let `echo x > <main>/PWNED` exit 0: the write landed in an ephemeral directory bwrap creates to hold its own binds. "The file did not appear" and "`git status` is unchanged" were both true, and both meant nothing. When a check confirms an absence, ask where the thing went — an escape that lands somewhere invisible passes every assertion written about the place it was supposed to reach.
- **Fixing a rule where it was noticed — a fourth time, in the file that warns about it.** `run-ticket` §0a says every path is relative to the ticket's own project *and quotes the cost of not doing so*; §5 and §7 then wrote literal `docs/tickets/FIX-NNN-…` and `cd server && ./build.sh build` anyway. Three separate sessions reported it, each time naming a different section, because each earlier fix repaired only the paragraph in front of it. A rule and its violations live in the same file more often than not: when correcting one, grep the file for the *pattern*, not the sentence.
- **A supervisor's own bookkeeping is not evidence about the world.** `killGroup` knew one pid and
  asserted that one pid. The process it leaked was the one it never had a number for — and that is
  the general case, not the unlucky one: whatever a supervisor has lost track of is exactly what
  its records cannot describe. When the claim is "nothing is left", the check has to enumerate the
  world (the process table, the port table, the open descriptors) and judge all of it. Counting
  what you already knew about always comes out even.
- **Something else was doing the cleaning up, and it has a race in it.** `killGroup` looked correct
  for months because `bwrap --die-with-parent` was tearing the sandbox down — a mechanism nobody
  had written down as load-bearing, armed a few tens of milliseconds after the spawn. When a
  function's job is being done by a side effect of somebody else's flag, it works until the timing
  changes. Ask what would happen if the helpful mechanism were absent; if the answer is "the same
  thing", it was not doing the job.
- **A measurement wide enough to close the window it is measuring.** The first draft of this
  ticket's positive case *passed against the defect*: it scanned `/proc` between the spawn and the
  release, and that scan costs ~13 ms of a window under 50. The instrument was the fix. When a test
  is about a race, everything it does between the trigger and the observation is part of the
  experiment — and a red run that comes back green is the result to investigate, not to accept.
- **A leak check that is machine-wide is a leak check that fails on a neighbour.** Three sessions
  ran their suites on this machine at once, and the global `pgrep -af bwrap` reported the others'
  healthy sandboxes as this run's leak. Scope the count by something only this run can produce, or
  the first false failure teaches everyone to ignore it.
- **A defect that exists only in the union of two branches.** Twice today: an implicit meaning for one field that two tickets widened differently, and eight documents that a new test on one branch rejected while the other branch was still writing them. Both branches were correct in isolation and both gates were honestly green, because each ran against a `main` that did not yet have the other's change. The only thing that finds this class is **running the full suite on `main` after the merge** — the `VERIFY` stage the template has always named and nobody was doing. A shared field with no written meaning is where it lands first.
- **An instrument slow enough to close the bug it is measuring.** `SRV-FIX-029`'s first positive case *passed* against a live defect: its `/proc` snapshot sat between the spawn and the release, and the scan costs ~13 ms of a window under 50 ms — so the measurement itself gave `--die-with-parent` time to clean up. A red run has to be read for *why*; a green one from a test you just wrote deserves the same suspicion. Take the snapshot **before** the thing you are timing, not inside it.
- **A negative test that survives a full revert is guarding something else.** `WEB-FIX-031`'s §1 mandated "fails when the fetch never resolves" — but a list that never answers fails an existence-wait as surely as a correct one, so reverting the entire fix would have left that case green. The defect needed a list that answers *late*. Choosing the break is the design work: revert the fix, and if the negative still passes, it never described the failure the fix prevents.
- **A constraint carried by an instruction instead of by a mechanism.** "Don't run the browser gate,
  five Chromes will make each other flaky" was true, and it turned five lanes into five lanes that
  could honestly report `GATE` without ever starting a browser. An instruction that has to be given,
  remembered and recorded degrades to nothing at the first busy day. If a resource cannot take five
  callers, make the resource queue them — then the lane never has to be told, and the check runs
  before the lane stops rather than after it merges.
- **A guarded section is an unrun check wearing a passed check's clothes.** `if (silent) { … }` in a
  gate omits its block when the setup did not happen, and the summary still reads `n/n checks
  passed`. Every skip has to cost a failure and name its reason, or the most valuable assertions —
  the ones that need the most setup — are the ones that quietly stop running.
- **A test that only ever subscribes and then listens reads one path and calls it the contract.**
  A snapshot and a delta are two renderings of one concept, and a case that takes the snapshot only
  as a starting position never judges it. The assertion that catches the whole class is *read one
  value by both paths and compare them field for field* — not "the snapshot has the right number of
  members", which a list of the wrong shape satisfies exactly as well. Any new frame type that has a
  bulk form and an incremental form needs that comparison, and it should be written the same day the
  second form is.
- **A shape decision recorded in a report is not a check.** `SRV-FEAT-005` wrote down that a lane
  snapshot's `entries` were frames and not entries, and gave the reason — the next ticket would then
  need one code path. The next ticket wrote two. Nothing in the repository disagreed with either,
  because the note lived in prose. If a decision is worth writing in a report, the schema or a test
  has to be able to state it too; if neither can, that is the finding.
- **A schema field typed `object` is a hole with a name on it.** `snapshot.data` was declared as
  nothing more than "an object" in the very file whose purpose is to stop the server and the UI
  disagreeing, so twelve tickets of drift validated clean and the generated types described neither
  shape. A contract that names a field and not its contents proves only that somebody thought about
  the field.
- **Fixing the first cause of a symptom is when the second one becomes visible.** `SRV-FIX-049` and
  `WEB-FIX-051` are one blank screen with two independent causes in two layers, and the second could
  not be seen — or even suspected — until the first was gone. When a user-reported symptom is fixed,
  re-run the user's own journey before believing it; the gate assertion that reproduces the report
  literally is the one that finds out.
- **A failure message that pairs two readings taken at different moments.** 8f printed the button as
  it was BEFORE the clock advance next to the lane row as it was AFTER, and the pair read as
  "the server says silent, the button says Stop" — a live-state bug that did not exist. Two
  integration attempts diagnosed it from the code and both were wrong. When a message quotes two
  facts, say when each was measured, or it will be read as one fact about one instant.
